techniques including automating remediation.

Module 1 - Extending your Solution with Management Packs (30 minutes) Basic
Module 1 - Extending your Solution with Management Packs (30 minutes) Basic

[7]
Introduction
Introduction
Aria Operations provides monitoring capabilities for vSphere environments in a standard configuration. Modern enterprises include
several layers of infrastructure, such as compute, storage and network, as well as multiple applications and cloud resources.
Management Packs
Management Packs (also known as Integrations) allow you to extend the monitoring capabilities of Aria Operations in order to analyze
data from more of your environment. These packs allow you to correlate events across multiple tiers, and they provide Aria Operations
with additional visibility and alerting.
In this lesson, we will review some of the available management packs for Aria Operations, install a new management pack, and review
the new capabilities the management pack provides.
Upon completing this module, you will be able to:-
Upon completing this module, you will be able to:-
- Review installed Aria Operations Management Packs (Solutions)
- Install additional Management Packs
Lab Captain(s):
Lab Captain(s):
Thomas Kopton, Lead Solution Architect, Germany

Add a New Management Pack
Add a New Management Pack
The VMware Aria Operations Repository includes several integration options by default. However, the list of available integrations does
not include management packs for compute, storage, network, or applications. How can we extend Aria Operations beyond these pre-
installed integrations? We'll demonstrate that next.

ADD to add a new management pack to the Repository.

Install the .pak File
Install the .pak File
Once the PAK file has been selected, it can be uploaded and verified.

UPLOAD to upload and validate the PAK file. This process will take 1-2 minutes.

NEXT.

H A N D S - O N  L A B S  M A N U A L  |  1 8


=== PAGE 19 ===
The options above the UPLOAD button allow for overwriting of already installed PAK files, and to skip signature checking if needed.
Since management packs provide additional alerts, symptoms, and more, it is highly recommended to backup existing customized
content if reinstalling a management pack. As with out of the box Aria Operations content, default management content should not be
modified directly in order to avoid overwriting customizations when updating management packs.

View Provided Alert Definitions
View Provided Alert Definitions

Enter.

Conclusion
Conclusion
In this module, we reviewed pre-installed VMware Aria Operations Management Packs
Management Packs and we installed, configured, and reviewed the
Management Pack for Storage Devices. Management packs provide significant extensibility to Aria Operations, allowing for proactive
management of a multi-cloud enterprise.
VMware Aria Operations includes several pre-installed integrations (management packs) for VMware solutions. Some of these
HOL-2501-02-VCF-L_5.2: Getting More Out of It!
H A N D S - O N  L A B S  M A N U A L  |  3 0


=== PAGE 31 ===
management packs are activated by default, but others require activation after deploying Aria Operations. See the Connecting VMware
Aria Operations to Data Sources documentation page for a list of pre-installed management packs. In addition to the pre-installed
management packs, several additional VMware and third-party management packs can be downloaded from the VMware Marketplace
VMware Marketplace.

=== PAGE 32 ===
Module 2 - Monitoring and Troubleshooting Kubernetes (30 minutes) Basic
Module 2 - Monitoring and Troubleshooting Kubernetes (30 minutes) Basic
[34]
[34]
Introduction
Introduction
In Module 1
Module 1 we were given an overview of Aria Operations Management Packs and how they provide further extensibility for Aria
Operations.
In Module 2
Module 2 we will continue looking at Management Packs by diving deep into the VMware Aria Operations Management Pack for
VMware Aria Operations Management Pack for
Kubernetes
Kubernetes. During this module we will first take care of the prerequisites by install cAdvisor into an TKG cluster that already exists
within the IaaS Control Plane (formerly vSphere with Tanzu), then deploy and configure the Management Pack. Finally, we will review
the detail discovered by the Management Pack.
Lab Captain(s):
Lab Captain(s):
•Christopher Lewis, Lead Solution Architect, UK
[35]
[35]
VMware Aria Operations Management Pack for Kubernetes
VMware Aria Operations Management Pack for Kubernetes
As with other Management Packs, the VMware Aria Operations Management Pack for Kubernetes
VMware Aria Operations Management Pack for Kubernetes includes a combination of alerts,
dashboards, and other additional functionality in to Aria Operations
Aria Operations for monitoring Kubernetes conformant clusters.
The Management Pack supports various Kubernetes distributions, including:
•VMware Tanzu Kubernetes Grid (TKG) - including the VMware IaaS Control Plane (formerly vSphere with Tanzu)
•VMware Tanzu Kuberentes Grid Integrated (TKGi)
•Red Hat OpenShift
•Amazon EKS
•Any supported Kubernets (version dependent)
The Management Pack allows for complete visualization of all of your Kubernetes resources across multiple distributions, including:
•Cluster topology
•Namespaces
•Clusters
•Replica Sets
•Nodes
•Pods
•Containers
In addition, the Management Pack supports the autodiscovery of clusters when using Tanzu Kubernetes Grid, Tanzu Kubernetes Grid
Integrated and clusters provisioned within VMware Tanzu Mission Control.
The full official documentation for the VMware Aria Operations Management Pack for Kubernetes
VMware Aria Operations Management Pack for Kubernetes can be located on the VMware by
Broadcom documentation
documentation site.

Introduction
Introduction
In VMware Aria Operations, capacity settings play a crucial role in effectively managing and optimizing the utilization of our
infrastructure resources, which is essential for effective resource management and optimization.
We will provide an understanding of various capacity settings, so that we can ensure efficient utilization of infrastructure resources while
maintaining high availability and minimizing risks.
At the end of this lab, we will have discussed these topics:
•Capacity Models and Algorithms
•Configuring Policy Settings
•Criticality Thresholds
•Workload Optimization: Allocation Model vs. Demand Model
•Risk Level: Conservative vs. Aggressive
•HA and Buffers
•Business Hours
Remember, customizing these settings based on an organization's unique requirements is key to achieving optimal results.

Understanding Capacity Settings
Understanding Capacity Settings
This section focuses on Aria Operations Capacity Management.
Capacity Management. We will touch on capacity terminology, the capacity engine with its
HOL-2501-02-VCF-L_5.2: Getting More Out of It!
H A N D S - O N  L A B S  M A N U A L  |  8 0


=== PAGE 81 ===
inputs and outputs, projections based on different risk levels, and capacity policy.
Aria Operations uses an advanced capacity engine to predict resource needs based on industry data, preventing resource deficits.
With Aria Operations, we can view both current and projected capacity across our private cloud, and unlike vCenter's real-time
resource view, Aria Operations offers forward-looking analytics for proactive planning.
[96]
[96]
Capacity Definitions
Capacity Definitions
Definitions
Definitions
•Total Capacity:
Total Capacity: The sum of all the capacity available. All available resources (CPU, memory, disk space, IOPS) in the
environment.
•Overhead:
Overhead: The resources needed by the hypervisor to run the workload.
•High Availability (HA):
High Availability (HA): The capacity reserved due to vSphere HA Admission Control settings on the Cluster.
•Buffer:
Buffer: The amount of capacity reserved (after vSphere HA is applied).
•Usable Capacity after HA and Buffer:
Usable Capacity after HA and Buffer: The amount of capacity available after removing capacity needed for HA and Buffer.
•Usage/Utilization:
Usage/Utilization: The amount of capacity the workload receives.
•Demand:
Demand: The amount of capacity the workload is asking for. Demand equals Usage unless a resource is constrained.
•Contention:
Contention: Caused when Demand is greater than Usage/Utilization.
HOL-2501-02-VCF-L_5.2: Getting More Out of It!
H A N D S - O N  L A B S  M A N U A L  |  8 1


=== PAGE 82 ===
[97]
[97]
Capacity Engine and Calculations
Capacity Engine and Calculations
HOL-2501-02-VCF-L_5.2: Getting More Out of It!
H A N D S - O N  L A B S  M A N U A L  |  8 2


=== PAGE 83 ===
The capacity engine analyzes historical utilization and projects future workload by using real-time predictive capacity analytics, which is
based on an industry-standard statistical analysis model of demand behavior. The image shows how Demand and Usable Capacity are
used to estimate certain output metrics.
Input Metrics
Input Metrics
•Demand
Demand
•Usable Capacity
Usable Capacity
Output Metrics
Output Metrics
•Time Remaining
Time Remaining: The number of days remaining till the projected utilization crosses the threshold for the usable capacity (total
capacity excluding HA settings).
•Capacity Remaining
Capacity Remaining: The largest difference between the usable capacity and the projected utilization between now and 3
days into the future. If the projected utilization is above 100% of the usable capacity, the capacity remaining is 0.
•Recommended Size
Recommended Size: The maximum projected utilization (excludes HA settings) for a projection period of 150 days into the
future (default). The projection period runs from the current time to 30 days after the warning threshold value for time
remaining. The warning threshold is the period during which the time remaining is green and its default value is 120 days. Aria
Operations caps the recommended size generated by the capacity engine to keep the recommendations conservative:
▪Oversized
Oversized: Capped at 50% of currently allocated resources. Example: If a VM with 8 vCPUs has never used more
than 10% CPU historically, recommendation is to reclaim just 4 vCPUs, not 7.
▪Undersized
Undersized: Capped at 100% of currently allocated resources. Example: If a VM with 4 vCPUs has been constantly
running very hot historically, recommendation is to add 4 vCPUs, not 8.
•Recommended Total Capacity
Recommended Total Capacity: The maximum projected utilization (includes HA settings) for a projection period of 150 days
into the future (default).
Projection
Projection
Metrics help us with workload management, resource allocation, and capacity planning. The projection window for the capacity engine
is 1 year into the future. The engine consumes data points every 5 minutes to ensure real-time calculation of output metrics.
HOL-2501-02-VCF-L_5.2: Getting More Out of It!
H A N D S - O N  L A B S  M A N U A L  |  8 3


=== PAGE 84 ===
[98]
[98]
Projection - Conservative Risk
Projection - Conservative Risk
The figure shows the capacity calculations for a conservative risk level.
Conservative Risk Level
Conservative Risk Level
In Aria Operations, capacity calculations can be adjusted based on the desired risk level, allowing you to customize the level of
conservatism in capacity planning. Capacity calculations at a conservative risk level prioritize stability, ensuring ample resources for
future growth and potential spikes in demand. This setting takes a more cautious approach to capacity planning, allowing for more
headroom and buffer in resource allocations. It may result in lower consolidation ratios and higher resource overhead.
Conservative risk level settings can also be adjusted typically in the capacity planning or policy settings, where you can specify the
desired level of conservatism.
Upper Bound / Lower Bound Projection:
Upper Bound / Lower Bound Projection: Upper Bound Projection and Lower Bound Projection are concepts used in forecasting and
predicting future values or trends. The capacity engine projects the future workload in a range. Capacity calculations are based on the
time remaining and risk level. The engine considers the Upper Bound Projection for a conservative risk level and the mean of the Upper
Bound Projection and Lower Bound Projection for an aggressive risk level.
HOL-2501-02-VCF-L_5.2: Getting More Out of It!
H A N D S - O N  L A B S  M A N U A L  |  8 4


=== PAGE 85 ===
[99]
[99]
Projection - Aggressive Risk
Projection - Aggressive Risk
The figure shows the capacity calculations for an aggressive risk level.
Aggressive Risk Level
Aggressive Risk Level
•Capacity calculations at an aggressive risk level aim to maximize resource utilization and minimize overhead.
•This setting assumes a higher tolerance for risk and allows for more aggressive allocation of resources.
•It may result in higher consolidation ratios and tighter resource utilization.
Aggressive risk level settings can be adjusted typically in the capacity planning or policy settings, where you can specify the desired
level of aggressiveness.
HOL-2501-02-VCF-L_5.2: Getting More Out of It!
H A N D S - O N  L A B S  M A N U A L  |  8 5


=== PAGE 86 ===
[100]
[100]
Utilization Peaks
Utilization Peaks
HOL-2501-02-VCF-L_5.2: Getting More Out of It!
H A N D S - O N  L A B S  M A N U A L  |  8 6


=== PAGE 87 ===
The historical utilization of resources can have peaks, which are periods of maximum utilization. The projection of future workload
depends on the types of peaks. According to the frequency of peaks, they can be momentary, sustained, or periodic.
•Momentary Peaks:
Momentary Peaks: Short-lived peaks that are a one-time occurrence. The peaks are not significant enough to require
additional capacity, so they do not impact capacity planning and projection.
•Sustained Peaks:
Sustained Peaks: Peaks that last for a longer time and impact projections. If a sustained peak is not periodic, the impact on
the projection lessens over time because of exponential decay.
•Periodic Peaks:
Periodic Peaks: Peaks that exhibit cyclical patterns or waves. The peaks can be hourly, daily, weekly, monthly, during the last
day of the month, and so on. The capacity engine also detects multiple overlapping cyclical patterns.
Let's move from theory to practice. Can we find these settings in the Aria Operations GUI?

[101]

Policy Definition

Risk Level - Overview
Risk Level - Overview
We can set the risk level to determine when the forecasted utilization reaches usable capacity. For production and mission-critical
workloads, we select a more Conservative
Conservative calculation.
1. Ensure Peak focused
Peak focused is unselected. We select this option if we want to use only the upper range of the data. The projection
will be based on the high utilization points.
2.The Conservativeness Level can be tuned from 0-5, with level 0 being the least conservative and level 5 being the most
conservative. By default, the level of conservativeness is set to 3. Maintain the 'Conservativeness Level' at 3.
Rare and momentary peaks may be considered outliers and may not impact the projections, even at the most conservative risk level.
Conservativeness Strength
Conservativeness Strength is supported by the Conservative risk level.
The Upper Bound
Upper Bound will vary based on the Conservativeness level chosen. Modifying the latter will make the projection bounds narrower
or wider. The higher the level, the wider the bounds and more conservative the projections for Time Remaining, Capacity Remaining,
and Recommended Size.
HOL-2501-02-VCF-L_5.2: Getting More Out of It!

aggressive risk level.

Update Business Hours
Update Business Hours
We can set Business Hours schedule for VMs and clusters only. After we specify Business Hours, the capacity forecast for the objects
using the current policy will be based on the specified Business Hours and not on 24 hours.
1. For the item 'Business Hours', click the Expand Arrows
Expand Arrows.
2.Unlock 'Set Business Hours' by clicking the padlock.
padlock.
3.Under Monday, use the sliders to set the 'Business Hours' to: from 6:00 AM
6:00 AM (06:00) to 8:00 PM
8:00 PM (20:00).
4.Click SET ALL.
SET ALL.
We configure Business Hours according to our time zone for enhanced capacity analysis and projections in Aria Operations. Non-
business hours activities on VMs, such as OS upgrades or virus scans, can skew perceived idleness. By marking the Business Hours
schedule, off-hour metrics can be effectively analyzed for inventory, compliance, and troubleshooting. Analysis and recommendations
for reclamation and rightsizing are based on Business Hours, ignoring post-business hour spikes. Since the Business Hours schedules
are based on policies, different objects can have different Business Hours. The capacity charts will be based on Business Hours.
HOL-2501-02-VCF-L_5.2: Getting More Out of It!

=== PAGE 99 ===
We select an object type to view the policy elements and settings for it. Settings can be viewed and modified for Allocation Model,
Allocation Model,
Custom Profile and Capacity Buffer.
Custom Profile and Capacity Buffer.
•Demand Model:
Demand Model: The Demand Model is a concept used to predict future resource requirements and it is a key component of
capacity planning and optimization, allowing administrators to anticipate the growth of workloads and infrastructure resources
needed to support those workloads over time. The Demand Model takes into account various factors, such as historical
resource consumption, trends, seasonality, and potential changes in workloads.
•Allocation Model:
Allocation Model: The Allocation Model determines how much compute, memory, and storage resources are allocated to
object types. We define the allocation values by modifying the policy which is applied to the objects. The allocation values,
also known as overcommit ratios, affect performance and cost. The Allocation Model works alongside the Demand Model.
Unlike the Demand Model which always affects the capacity calculations (by default, only the Demand Model is used), the
Allocation Model can be turned on or off in the policy setting.
The Allocation Model differs from the Demand Model as it does not consider Utilization. It is simply based on configured resources (e.g.,
4 vCPU, 16 GB virtual RAM, 100 GB virtual Disk) allocated to VMs rather than physical resources used by VMs. As a result, the Demand
Model is still considered when Allocation is enabled.
•Custom Profile:
Custom Profile: Custom Profile is a user defined VM profile, used to determine the number of VMs which will fit within the
remaining capacity.
•Capacity Buffer:
Capacity Buffer: The Capacity Buffer reduces the usable capacity. In a vSphere Cluster, it is applied after High Availability
(HA). The Capacity Buffer is meant to be used as a safety net and is a reserved resource pool to handle unexpected demand,
reducing usable capacity but enhancing reliability. It helps mitigate risk during resource surges and aids accurate capacity
planning by reflecting a realistic view of available resources. Buffer size should balance between accommodating unexpected
demand and not overly reducing usable capacity.
HOL-2501-02-VCF-L_5.2: Getting More Out of It!
H A N D S - O N  L A B S  M A N U A L  |  9 9

=== PAGE 102 ===
[117]
[117]
Criticality Thresholds
Criticality Thresholds
Criticality Thresholds
Criticality Thresholds allow us to define when an alert should be triggered for a particular symptom based on the severity of a condition.
In the policy settings, we set thresholds for each metric Aria Operations collects. Each metric has five threshold levels: Info, Warning,
Info, Warning,
Immediate, Critical, and Alert
Immediate, Critical, and Alert. When the value of a particular metric crosses a defined threshold, an alert is triggered. The severity level
of the alert corresponds to the severity of the threshold level that the metric has crossed. Criticality Thresholds allow us to fine-tune our
policies so that we are alerted for potential issues at the right time, so that they can be addressed before they have a significant impact
on performance.
1. For the item 'Criticality Thresholds', click the Expand Arrows
Expand Arrows.
2.Click Cluster Compute Resource.
Cluster Compute Resource.
3.Unlock the items 'Time Remaining', 'Capacity Remaining' and 'Workload' by clicking the padlock
padlock icons on the left of each
item.
HOL-2501-02-VCF-L_5.2: Getting More Out of It!
H A N D S - O N  L A B S  M A N U A L  |  1 0 2


=== PAGE 103 ===
Threshold Levels
Threshold Levels
•Info:
Info: This threshold is used for informational purposes and typically does not trigger an alert.
•Warning
Warning: This threshold is set to a value where the system is still functioning normally, but the value of the metric is moving
towards a level where it could impact performance.
•Immediate
Immediate: This threshold level indicates that the system might soon experience performance issues if the metric value
continues to rise.
•Critical
Critical: At this threshold level, the system is likely experiencing performance issues. The issues should be addressed
immediately to prevent further degradation of performance.
•Alert
Alert: This is the highest threshold level. At this level, the system is likely experiencing severe performance issues.
HOL-2501-02-VCF-L_5.2: Getting More Out of It!
H A N D S - O N  L A B S  M A N U A L  |  1 0 3


=== PAGE 104 ===

Update Criticality Thresholds
Update Criticality Thresholds
Time Remaining
Time Remaining is how many days we have until the utilization projection crosses the usable capacity threshold. As we have already
seen, it has 2 settings: Conservative and Aggressive.
Capacity Remaining
Capacity Remaining is the % of usable capacity not consumed.
Workload
Workload is the immediate % of capacity consumed of the most constrained of several key resource containers. Since workload
changes every collection cycle, we can set how many cycles it takes to trigger or clear an alert.
1. Under 'Time Remaining', click the red Critical slider
red Critical slider and slide it to the left.
2.Stop at 15
15. Alternatively, on the 'Critical' field to the right, type 15.

[125]
[125]
Capacity Management: Added Value of Aria Operations
Capacity Management: Added Value of Aria Operations
Aria Operations is focused on capacity planning and ensuring we have sufficient resources to meet our workload needs now and in the
future, taking into account buffers for workload spikes and HA failovers. vSphere/vCenter are more operationally focused, detailing how
resources are allocated and used at a given point in time. While vSphere/vCenter give us the real-time operational perspective, Aria
Operations adds the predictive analytics and capacity planning layer that helps us stay proactive and plan for the future.

[126]

Aria Operations:

=== PAGE 111 ===
[127]
[127]
Conclusion
Conclusion
Understanding capacity settings in Aria Operations is crucial for effective resource management and optimization. By comprehending
capacity models and algorithms, configuring policy settings, setting criticality thresholds, selecting appropriate allocation and demand
models, determining risk levels, ensuring high availability and buffers, and considering business hours, we can unlock the full potential
of our infrastructure resources while minimizing risks.
Customizing these settings to an organization's unique requirements is key to achieving optimal results.

=== PAGE 112 ===
Module 4 - Integrating and Troubleshooting with Logs (45 minutes) Advanced
Module 4 - Integrating and Troubleshooting with Logs (45 minutes) Advanced
[130]
[130]
Introduction
Introduction
VMware Aria Operations for Logs is commonly integrated with VMware Aria Operations for centralized log management and analysis,
and creates a seamless and robust troubleshooting process. By combining log data with metrics from Aria Operations, we efficiently
pinpoint the exact cause of issues, resulting in reduced time-to-resolution.
Aria Operations can enable proactive measures through predefined remediation steps based on logs, addressing specific conditions
proactively. By installing and configuring a linux agent you can get detailed information from enterprise business applications running
on servers with applications for finance, HR, ERP, CRM, mail, web servers, databases, proxy, VPN, authentication, security, backup or
simply desktops.
Upon completing this module, you will be able to:
•Troubleshoot with logs within Aria Operations
•Install and configure a Linux Agent
•Use filters to identify data sources and to isolate references to errors and warnings
Lab Captain(s):
Lab Captain(s):
•Bastian Beuggert, Lead Solution Architect, Switzerland

=== PAGE 127 ===
[149]
[149]
Configure liagent.ini
Configure liagent.ini
After the installation, we need to configure the agent to communicate with our Aria Operation for Logs server. Typically, this involves
specifying the server's hostname and the port for sending logs. We are also specifying that we're using SSL with no certificate check in
our lab, and that we would like a central configuration.
1. Type or copy/paste
copy/paste each of these lines of code, and then press ENTER
ENTER after each line of code:
2.sed -i 's/;proto=cfapi/proto=cfapi/g' /var/lib/loginsight-agent/liagent.ini
3.sed -i 's/;port=9543/port=9543/g' /var/lib/loginsight-agent/liagent.ini
4.sed -i '1,// s/;ssl=yes/ssl=yes/g' /var/lib/loginsight-agent/liagent.ini
5.sed -i 's/;ssl=yes/ssl_accept_any=yes/g' /var/lib/loginsight-agent/liagent.ini
6.sed -i 's/;central_config=yes/central_config=yes/g' /var/lib/loginsight-agent/liagent.ini
7./etc/init.d/liagentd restart
These Steps concludes our Agent Configuration on the Ubuntu Machine, as a next Step, we will configure the Agent Configuration in the
Aria Operations for Logs GUI.

Agents Configuration
Agents Configuration
Once the agent is connected to the Aria Operations for Logs server, the next step is to configure log filtering. This ensures that we're
monitoring only the relevant logs. This is crucial to avoid overwhelming the Aria Operations for Logs server with unnecessary data. Aria
Operations for Logs uses the concept of "Content Packs" that define parsing rules for different log sources. Aria Operations for Logs'
filtering capabilities is used to specify which logs should be forwarded from the agent to the server. This can be based on log levels,
keywords, or any other relevant criteria.
1. In Aria Operation for Logs, Expand
Expand the left menu Click '>>'.
'>>'.
2.Click Management.
Management.
3.Click Agents.
Agents.
4.Check
Check if the Agent on our Linux System ubuntu-0001218.vcf.holo.lab
ubuntu-0001218.vcf.holo.lab is visible.
Note: We see a list of all OS and agents, both Linux and Windows that are running on the operating systems.

=== PAGE 166 ===
To imitate artificial log data from a financial application called The Hands-On Labs Application
The Hands-On Labs Application and put the various error messages into
the Application Event log on the windows server, we will now run a script that will automatically generate log messages coming from
our financial application for this exercise.
1. From the Terminal Server Console, on windows-0001202
windows-0001202, open Windows Explorer
Windows Explorer.
2.Navigate to the folder: C:\LabFiles
C:\LabFiles.
3.Right-click the file hol_financial_app.cmd
hol_financial_app.cmd.
4.Select Run as administrator
Run as administrator. A command prompt window will appear on screen, display some text and then automatically
close after a few seconds. This is expected behavior, you are not expected to read the text within the command prompt
window.
For future reference, here is the entire 'hol_financial_app.cmd
hol_financial_app.cmd' file content:
@echo off
setlocal enabledelayedexpansion
:: # Module 4 - Integrating and Troubleshooting with Logs
:: # Setting error messages and source (application) in pseudo-arrays.
set "Error[1]=Could not open Bank File bank_id.txt"
set "Error[2]=Uunable to communicate with the main bank server"
set "Error[3]=The main communication service has stopped"
set "Error[4]=Unable to connect to the database bank_transaction.db"
set "Error[5]=Failed to generate the financial report for current Quarter"
set "Error[6]=Balance sheet calculation internal error"
set "Error[7]=Tax calculation service is not responding"
set "Application=The Hands-On Labs Application (Fake HOL Financial app)"
:: # Generating errors in the event log.
for /L %%i in (1,1,10) do (
:: # Generate two random numbers.
set /A "randError=!random! %% 7 + 1"
set /A "randEventID=!random! %% 100 + 1"
:: # Use 'call' to indirectly reference array element
call set "errorMessage=%%Error[!randError!]%%"
:: # Create the event, including the application name and "ERROR, " in the error message.
eventcreate /ID !randEventID! /L APPLICATION /T ERROR /SO "!Application!" /D "!Application! ERR
)
HOL-2501-02-VCF-L_5.2: Getting More Out of It!
H A N D S - O N  L A B S  M A N U A L  |  1 6 6

Each additional time we ran the script would add 10 more events. As well as the axis on the left showing the value of 10 we
can also hoover the mouse pointer over the chart which will generate a pop up box containing the number of events.
2.From the bottom pane, we see the details, such as the source log channel (Application, System, or Security
Application, System, or Security), the provider
name, the source IP address and more.
3.If we wanted to investigate our logs even further, the best tool for doing so is of course Aria Operations for Logs. The tool can
be launched directly from here by clicking LAUNCH OPERATIONS FOR LOGS.
LAUNCH OPERATIONS FOR LOGS.

Introduction

your organization's needs. You will be equipped with the knowledge to track expenses, calculate costs, and analyze cost data

Lab Captain(s):
Lab Captain(s):
•Cosmin Trif, Solution Architect, US
[213]
[213]
Pricing and Calculation
Pricing and Calculation
Depreciation is like splitting the cost of a pricey item, like a server, over the years it's used. Instead of saying we spent all the money in
year one, we spread the cost over its lifespan. Each year, we "use up" some of the server's value. It's like slicing a cake over several
days, so everyone gets a piece when they want it. This gives a better picture of yearly expenses, reflecting when we're actually using the
server.

Updating Cost Settings
Updating Cost Settings
We buy a server for $9,999. We expect it to last for 3 years, and after that, it will have no resale value (salvage value) meaning that after
3 years, the server's book value is $0, which matches its expected resale value. Using the straight-line depreciation method, we
calculate the annual depreciation:
1. Change the Depreciation Years to 3
2.For the Depreciation model, Choose Straight Line
Straight Line
3.Click Save
Save
(Cost of Server - Salvage Value) / Useful Life in Years
= ($9,999 - $0) / 3
= $3,333 per year

=== PAGE 213 ===
[238]
[238]
Showback
Showback
Showback offers a transparent representation of resource usage costs, enabling stakeholders to understand and optimize their IT
investments.
Showback as a mechanism allows us to give god visibility of the usage and costs of resources and services utilized by various
departments, business units or projects. Showback is not Chargeback or billing, so we use it to promote responsibility and
accountability.
With Showback we demystify resource consumption, and it gives us awareness around the use of IT resources.
[239]
[239]
Cost and Showback
Cost and Showback
Quantifying costs and implementing showback practices are vital in cloud-based IT environments. Continuously analyzing running VM
costs and implementing showback practices optimize resource allocation, improve cost-efficiency, and promote transparency in cloud-
based IT environments.
1. Cost Analysis:
Cost Analysis: Cost refers to expenses incurred in running VMs within the cloud, particularly in IaaS (Infrastructure as a
Service.) Costs can be based on utilization or allocation of resources.
2.Showback:
Showback: Reveals VM costs to enhance transparency and accountability. It provides cost visibility, fosters transparency and
accountability, and facilitates informed decision-making.
HOL-2501-02-VCF-L_5.2: Getting More Out of It!
H A N D S - O N  L A B S  M A N U A L  |  2 1 3


=== PAGE 214 ===
[240]
[240]
Price and Chargeback
Price and Chargeback
Accurately determining prices and implementing chargeback processes are crucial for billing
billing customers based on their virtual machine
(VM) usage.
Price:
Price: Price represents the amount charged to customers for utilizing a VM. The price of a VM most often differ from its actual
operational costs, as additional charges or profit margins (up-charge) is included. Price considerations are often specific to VMs, and
customers are typically billed on a monthly basis.
Chargeback:
Chargeback: Involves generating a bill for customers based on the determined price for their VM usage. The bill should include a
breakdown of the charges associated with each VM, reflecting the price per month.
[241]
[241]

Go to Cost

savings, and trends related to the VMs in the selected group.
The "Showback (VM Cost)" dashboard is a pre-built tool designed to track and analyze virtual machine (VM) costs. It offers an overview
of the costs associated with VMs in a group, allowing for quick assessment. To enhance cost accuracy, the cost drivers can be edited,
although customization options are only available in the Advanced or Enterprise edition of VMware Aria Operations. This dashboard
provides comprehensive information on VM costs, including total costs, cost distribution by resource groups or subscriptions, cost
trends over time, top VM costs, and analyses based on VM size. Its purpose is to help identify areas of high costs, optimize spending,
and enable informed decision-making to enhance cost efficiency and resource allocation.

=== PAGE 225 ===
Cost Drivers:
Cost Drivers: Cost Drivers are the aspect that contributes to the expense of your business operations. Cost drivers provide a link
between a pool of costs. Cost Drivers help you with expense management. There are various cost drivers such as Server Hardware,
Maintenance Contracts, Facilities like real estate, Power and Cooling, Network, Storage, Labor and even licenses like OS, VMware
software, SQL Server etc. You can set a total cost for the License, Labor, Network, Maintenance, and facilities cost drivers in VMware
Aria Operations.
Cost Reference Database:
Cost Reference Database: According to the industry standard, VMware Aria Operations maintains a reference cost for these cost
drivers, meaning that Cost Drivers are populated by default using the Cost Reference Database that ships with Aria Operations. If we
modify the reference cost of cost drivers in VMware Aria Operations, we override the values in the reference database.

[255]

View

Changing Labor Values
Changing Labor Values
Within our organization, we possess a group of individuals who specialize in hardware management and boast exceptional proficiency
in vSphere, surpassing the capabilities of VI Admins. Among our pool of employees, there is an abundance of expertise in the Windows
operating system; however, we face a significant deficit in Linux knowledge. Consequently, our administrators frequently resort to
online searches for resolving Ubuntu-related queries. The scarcity of Linux knowledge within our organization results in higher labor
costs associated with Linux expertise. In contrast, expenses related to other areas remain comparatively lower. This discrepancy arises
due to the need for additional time and resources allocated by our administrators to research and resolve Ubuntu-related issues,
resulting in increased expenses.
1. Change the Monthly hours of labor per Linux Instance from 0.04 to 0.08
from 0.04 to 0.08
2.Change the Linux Labor hourly rate to 40
40
3.Change the Monthly hours of labor per Windows Desktop Instance from 0.04 to 0.02
from 0.04 to 0.02
4.Change the Monthly hours of labor per Windows Server Instance from 0.04 to 0.02
from 0.04 to 0.02
5.Change the Monthly hours of labor per VM from 0.04 to 0.03
from 0.04 to 0.03
6.Change the Virtual Infrastructure/ VM Labor hourly rate from 49.4 to 45
from 49.4 to 45
7.Click Save
Save

Using Symptoms and Alerts to Trigger Recommendations and Actions
Using Symptoms and Alerts to Trigger Recommendations and Actions
VMware Aria Operations Alerts are similar to rules used for years in monitoring critical IT resources. However, previous rule-based
HOL-2501-02-VCF-L_5.2: Getting More Out of It!
H A N D S - O N  L A B S  M A N U A L  |  2 4 3


=== PAGE 244 ===
systems tended to be static and difficult to build, deploy, and maintain. Aria Operations leverages built-in analytics and pre-defined
content to provide a dynamic, effective, and scalable approach for identifying and resolving issues in our environment.
For this lesson, we will start by creating a Symptom Definition. Symptom Definitions enable Aria Operations to identify problems with
objects in our environment. These Symptom Definitions will then trigger Alerts when conditions qualify as problems. In this scenario,
the condition to monitor is the high CPU workload on the virtual machine "ubuntu-000xxx". Creating one or more of the Symptoms
enables them to be added to an Alert Definition. When all symptoms contributing to the alert definitions are triggered, Aria Operations
will then issue an alert. In this lesson, we'll go through this in more detail and we'll also show how we can automate certain actions to
occur when an alert is triggered in Aria Operations.
Due to the dynamic nature of this lab environment and the host naming policy applied to deployed resources, the 6 numbers within the
name of a virtual machine may differ between the instructions provided, what is shown within the associated picture and the lab
environment.
[280]
[280]
Creating Custom Alerts
Creating Custom Alerts
HOL-2501-02-VCF-L_5.2: Getting More Out of It!
H A N D S - O N  L A B S  M A N U A L  |  2 4 4


=== PAGE 245 ===
Symptom Definitions evaluate conditions in our environment that, if true, trigger a symptom and can result in a generated alert. We can
add symptom definitions that are based on metrics or super metrics, properties, message events, fault events, or metric events. We can
create a symptom definition as we create an alert definition or as an individual item in the appropriate symptom definition list.
1. Click the chevron
chevron to expand Operations, if needed to show the configuration options.

Symptom Definitions box .

2.

Alert Definition
Alert Definition
An alert definition is comprised of one or more symptom definitions, and the alert definition can be associated with a one or multiple
recommendations which may include actions that help you resolve the problem. Alert definitions include triggering symptom definitions
and actionable recommendations. We create the alert definitions so that the generated alerts tell us about problems in the monitored
environment. We can then respond to the alerts with effective solutions that are provided in the recommendations.
To create Alert Definitions:
1. Click Configurations
Configurations to go back to the Configurations Main Page.
2.Click Alert Definitions
Alert Definitions.

Alert Impact
Alert Impact
Alert Impact settings and their definitions are shown below. These settings determine how our alert will be classified and triggered.

•The Impact
Impact field will categorize the alert as a health, risk, or efficiency problem.
•The Criticality
Criticality field shows how serious the problem is.
For Criticality, we can select one of the following values:
For Criticality, we can select one of the following values:
•Info (informational purposes only; does not affect badge colors)
•Warning (lowest level; displays yellow)
•Immediate (medium level; displays orange)
•Critical (highest level; displays red)
•The Alert Type
Alert Type and Subtype
Subtype fields can be used to classify the alert; an example would be using these fields' information to
route the alert to the appropriate personnel in our organization when using Notifications
Notifications
Finally, choose settings for our cycle, which are data collection intervals.
Finally, choose settings for our cycle, which are data collection intervals.
•Wait Cycle
Wait Cycle indicates how many cycles should pass before triggering the alert
•Cancel Cycle
Cancel Cycle indicates how many cycles without symptoms should pass before the alert is canceled

=== PAGE 281 ===
[315]
[315]
Go to Custom Group
Go to Custom Group
VMware Aria Operations Custom Groups
Custom Groups are a means to group objects, even of different types, according to specific rules in order to
use them later in dashboards, reports, etc.
In essence, Custom Groups provide a flexible mechanism to logically organize objects across our environment and customize how Aria
Operations handles those grouped objects through applied policies. This allows for more granular and targeted monitoring, alerting,
and reporting capabilities.
HOL-2501-02-VCF-L_5.2: Getting More Out of It!

alert will be generated. (NOTE: It may take a few minutes for the VM Health Badge to turn red).
2.Notice the increased CPU usage on the graph.

=== PAGE 322 ===
Module 7 - Creating Views for Better Visibility (15 minutes) Basic
Module 7 - Creating Views for Better Visibility (15 minutes) Basic
[364]
[364]
Introduction
Introduction
Reports and Dashboards are the key to understanding and visualizing the environment that is being monitored. The key component of a
Report or a Dashboard is a View. A View helps you interpret data (such as metrics, properties, policies and symptoms) from a number
of perspectives. Those perspectives can be transformed to highlight how the data has historically changed (trend) or how the data may
look in the future (forecast) built on the historical trend.In this module we will walk through the creation of custom views in Aria
Operations. Successfully creating custom views will ensure we can use Aria Operations to track what is important/critical to the
monitoring of our VMware Cloud Infrastructure.
The following lessons are within this Module:
•Create Customized View
•Simple View showing VM list with Metrics and Properties
•Create a View with Variable data
•Create a View with Trends
•Create a View with Distribution data
•Create Reports from Views and Dashboards
[365]
[365]
Create Simple View showing VM list with Metrics and Properties
Create Simple View showing VM list with Metrics and Properties
In this lesson, we will create a view. A view can be used in dashboards and reports. A view is also viewable as its own content in the
Details section of the Aria Operations interface.
The view for this lesson is a starting point and intended to be a simple example to create. It will contain some basic metrics and
properties for virtual machines.
HOL-2501-02-VCF-L_5.2: Getting More Out of It!

=== PAGE 366 ===
We now have a view that shows us the last CPU Demand collected for each Powered ON Virtual Machine. We also show the Maximum
CPU Demand as a percentage for the last 30 days. The last value in our view shows us what the Ready % was when the demand was at
maximum during the same 30 day period.
This is a very powerful feature of the product. While we are showing the ready % when the CPU is highly demanded, you may wish to
see what disk latency looks like when network transmissions are high. You can correlate any two metrics that are being collected in
vRealize Operations.

=== PAGE 434 ===
We have the ability to include the following:
•Cover Page
•Table of contents
•Footer
For a Cover Page:
•Can contain an image up to 5 MB.
•The default report size is 8.5 inches by 11 inches. The image is resized to fit the report front page.
Table of Contents
•Provides a list of the template parts, organized in the order of their appearance in the report
Footer
•Includes the date when the report is created, a note that the report is created by VMware vRealize Operations Manager, and
page number.
Don't make any changes here, we will use the default settings.
[478]
[478]
Portrait or Landscape
Portrait or Landscape
Each view and dashboard can be oriented to portrait or landscape mode. For dashboards in a report, landscape will likely be a better
choice to simulate the aspect ratio of a monitor. Some dashboards require scrolling. When a dashboard is too large to be displayed on
the screen, it will not fit into a report very well either. Make sure Assess Cost is set to Landscape.

Module 9 - Advanced Troubleshooting Techniques (15 minutes) Intermediate
Module 9 - Advanced Troubleshooting Techniques (15 minutes) Intermediate
[537]
[537]
Introduction
Introduction
In VMware Aria Operations, the Troubleshooting Workbench is where we perform advanced troubleshooting tasks on an alert that was
triggered on an object. We can investigate both known and unknown issues in Aria Operations. The troubleshooting capability was
designed to focus in and out of an object to quickly identify if there is an issue with a specific object or, by providing the ability to zoom
out the scope, to see if there is a systemic issue within the infrastructure.
Lab Captain(s):
Lab Captain(s):
•Eirini Spanou, Senior Solution Architect, UK
•Bastian Beuggert, Lead Solution Architect, Switzerland
Due to the dynamic nature of this lab environment and the host naming policy applied to deployed resources, the 6 numbers within the
name of a virtual machine may differ between the instructions provided, what is shown within the associated picture and the lab
environment.
[538]
[538]
Log in to Aria Operations
Log in to Aria Operations
In the following few pages, we will walk through the process for logging in to Aria Operations
Aria Operations.

Firefox.

Firefox icon on the Windows Quick Launch Task Bar.

=== PAGE 485 ===
In the Troubleshooting Workbench, we look for potential evidence of a problem within a specific scope and time range. The Selected
Scope control on the left of the page is where we vary the scope. We can vary the scope in the following ways:
•We can select only the object that we are investigating
select only the object that we are investigating, or include several upstream and downstream relationships by
increasing the scope. As we increase the scope, more objects are displayed in the inventory tree.
•We can select a custom scope
select a custom scope to include objects of our choice. We can click CUSTOM
CUSTOM to open an interactive window where
we use the pointer to visually rearrange our objects, view relationships and add peers to modify the relationships. To see
details about the object, place the pointer for a few seconds above the object. We can reset a custom scope to start all over
again.
•We can use the drop-down menu to narrow down the type of objects
narrow down the type of objects displayed.
1. To expand the Selected Scope one more level (add an additional layer of parent and child objects), click the '+'
'+' icon.
In this example, we have selected the object under investigation (ubuntu-xxx VM) and have increased the scope by increasing the
Scope Level from 1 to 2.

Potential Evidence Tab
Potential Evidence Tab
HOL-2501-02-VCF-L_5.2: Getting More Out of It!
H A N D S - O N  L A B S  M A N U A L  |  4 9 4


=== PAGE 495 ===
The potential evidence is based on Events, Property Changes, and Anomalous Metrics, which are displayed on the right pane of the
Troubleshooting Workbench under the Potential Evidence tab. Information in these sections is displayed as cards. This is a purpose
built collection of data points to highlight critical details relating to an object that could help us identify problem areas quickly.
Three main pillars of Events
Events, Property Changes
Property Changes and Anomalous Metrics
Anomalous Metrics are highlighted and driven by the Scope that is defined. Note
that much of the evidence shown is on the parent object, the host system, esxi-xxx (name can differ). The Potential Evidence pane will
automatically adjust as we zoom the Scope in and out.
•Events:
Events: Displays events, based on a change in the metrics. Events for metrics that have breached the usual behaviour, and
major events that have occurred within the selected scope and time are displayed. The cards are based on dynamic
thresholds for a metric, which are calculated based on historical and incoming data.
•Property Changes:
Property Changes: Displays important configuration changes that occurred within the selected scope and time. Both single
and multiple property changes are displayed. For multiple property changes, we can view the latest and previous changes.
•Anomalous Metrics:
Anomalous Metrics: Metrics which have shown drastic changes within the selected scope and time. Ranks the results based
on the degree of change. The most recent anomalous metric based on a time-sliced comparison in the current time range is
given the highest weightage.

[556]

Alerts Tab - Alerts

Alerts Tab - Symptoms
Alerts Tab - Symptoms
Let's stay on the Alerts Tab and move on to explore Symptoms.
Symptoms are conditions that indicate problems in our environment. We can define symptoms in Aria Operations and add them to alert
definitions so that we know when a problem occurs with our monitored objects. As data is collected from the latter, the data is
compared to the defined symptom condition. If the condition is true, then the symptom is triggered.
1. Click Symptoms
Symptoms.
2.To expand the Scope to Level 2, click the '+'
'+' icon. (clear the custom filter if the level cannot be changed)
3.To explore Critical Symptoms and investigate in detail, click the > in front of the group 'Critical'.
Notice that, following Step 2, the number of Symptoms grew after we increased the Selected Scope from Level 1 to Level 2.

Alerts Tab - Events
Alerts Tab - Events
Let's stay on the Alerts Tab and move on to explore Events.
An Event is any change to an object defined by a change in the Metrics for that object. We can compare changes to an object with
Symptoms and other data to identify a possible cause for a generated Alert.
1. To show the Events within the defined Scope, click Events.
Events.
2.On the right of the Events pane, click the filter
filter icon.
3.To view the Event Type options, click the down arrow
down arrow next to the 'Event Type' field.
4.To hide the Event Type options (without selecting any one option), click the down arrow
down arrow next to the 'Event Type' field.
5.To extract the inactive Events, click the down arrow
down arrow next to the 'Status' field.
6.Click Inactive
Inactive.
7.To view the Criticality options, click the down arrow
down arrow next to the 'Criticality' field.
8.Click Critical
Critical.
9.Click APPLY
APPLY.

=== PAGE 499 ===
Notice that, for Events, Aria Operations provides the ability to filter on Event Type, Status, Criticality, Created On
Event Type, Status, Criticality, Created On and Canceled On
Canceled On to
focus our troubleshooting efforts.
Event Type:
Event Type: Describes the type of event that triggered on the selected object, and helps us categorize the event so that we can assign
certain types of events to specific system administrators.
Possible values include:
•Dynamic Threshold Violation
•Hard Threshold Violation
•Data Availability
•System Degradation
•Environment
•Change
•Notification
•Fault
•Property Change
•Comparison Symptom
Criticality
Criticality: Criticality is the level of importance of the event in our environment. The level is based on the level assigned when the event
definition was created, or, on the highest symptom criticality, if the assigned level was Symptom Based.
Possible values include:
•Info
•Warning
•Immediate
•Critical

Introduction to Automation Central
Introduction to Automation Central
Automation Central is a feature in VMware Aria Operations that allows you to create one-time or recurring jobs to automate actions
such as reclaiming or rightsizing VMs and run custom workflows created in VMware Orchestrator. Once you set up recurring jobs, you
can track and obtain reports on them and can customize jobs so that they only run based on certain parameters. For example, if you
choose to delete a snapshot as an action, you can specify how old the snapshot must be before it is deleted.
View reclamation and rightsizing reports through the Report Tab.
1. Click Report.
Report.
2.Click Reclamation
Reclamation to view the reclamation report. The reclamation report displays graphical and numerical data on the total
cost savings, CPUs reclaimed, memory reclaimed and storage reclaimed for different time periods.
3.Click the Rightsizing
Rightsizing reporting to view the rightsizing report. The rightsizing reports displays graphical and numerical data on
the CPUs downsized, memory downsized, CPU oversized, and memory upsized for different time periods.
Note: For each job, you also have the ability to view and change the scope of the reporting as well as the date range. For date ranges,
you can pick the last 4 quarters or last quarter.
HOL-2501-02-VCF-L_5.2: Getting More Out of It!
H A N D S - O N  L A B S  M A N U A L  |  5 1 1

Creating a Job from a Virtual Machine Rightsizing Report
Creating a Job from a Virtual Machine Rightsizing Report
One of the most common tasks is to create Job from Reclaim or Rightsizing reports. You can create an automation job based on the
recommendation provided by VMware Aria Operations in the Reclaim or Rightsizing pages.
In this exercise we'll work on creating a task to rightsize our Virtual Machines on a scheduled basis. Let's get started.

Introduction
Introduction
Rightsizing, identifying and fixing issues with oversized and undersized VMs, optimizes resource allocation. It maximizes performance
efficiency, reduces costs, and ensures seamless alignment with requirements. With well-defined policies and automation, rightsizing
becomes a proactive practice for VMware Aria Operation practitioners.
Upon completing this module, you will be able to:
•Using Rightsizing based on Aria Operations Recommendations
•Configure Policies:
◦Criticality Thresholds
◦Risk Level (Conservative vs. Aggressive)
◦Business Hours
•Automate Rightsizing

Lab Captain(s):
•Bastian Beuggert, Lead Solution Architect, Switzerland

Please note:
Please note: Many customers often hesitant to make significant alterations to their virtual machines (VMs) and prefer a more
cautious approach. The Recommended Size feature has been purposefully designed to adopt a conservative stance in its
suggestions. For VMs that are oversized, the Recommended Size is limited to a maximum of 50% of their current
configuration. This gradual approach aims to guide VMs toward their optimal size without proposing drastic changes
without proposing drastic changes, such as
reducing the number of virtual CPUs from 32 to just 1.

NEXT.
Aria Operations does not check if the VM hot add/remove setting is enabled. If the VM power off is not allowed, then the action will fail.
Note: If the number of resources for a job is ten or less, then the job runs at once. If the number of resources is more than ten, then the
jobs run in groups of ten, in parallel.

Additional &amp; Final thoughts
Additional &amp; Final thoughts
If we see that the resizing from Aria Operations failed with an error: "Unable to shut down or power off vm. VMware Tools not installed"
the reason why is that the Shutdown VM action requires the VMware Tools installed and running on the target virtual machines. If you
ran the action on more than one object, then VMware Tools was not installed, or installed but not running, on at least one of the virtual
machines.
Since this is a requirement for Aria Operations to run Actions on virtual machines in a vCenter, we need to ensure that VMware Tools is
installed and running on the managed virtual machines in the vCenter.
This will enable you to perform actions on the virtual machines using Aria Operations without any further issues related to VMware Tools
requirements.

analytics behind rightsizing leverage historical data, statistical models, and machine learning to ensure accurate recommendations

[642]
Recommended Size Calculation
Recommended Size Calculation
HOL-2501-02-VCF-L_5.2: Getting More Out of It!
H A N D S - O N  L A B S  M A N U A L  |  5 7 0


=== PAGE 571 ===
The capacity engine of Aria Operations analyzes historical utilization and projects future workload by using real-time predictive capacity
analytics, which is based on an industry-standard statistical analysis model of demand behavior. As shown in the figure, the engine
takes the Demand and Usable Capacity metrics as input and generates the output metrics, which are Time Remaining, Capacity
Remaining, Recommended Size, and Recommended Total Capacity.
Recommended Size (optimal resource configuration)
Recommended Size (optimal resource configuration)
Determined by projecting utilization for a specific period. It extends 30 days beyond the warning threshold, which is the green period
for time remaining. The recommended size does not include HA settings. By default, if the warning threshold is set at 120 days, the
recommended size reflects the maximum projected utilization 150 days ahead. To maintain conservative recommendations, Aria
Operations imposes caps on the recommended size generated by the capacity engine.
Oversized
Oversized: Aria Operations limits oversized recommendations to 50% of the current allocation. For instance, if a virtual machine with
8 vCPUs historically only used up to 10% CPU, the recommendation is capped at reclaiming 4 vCPUs rather than 7.
Undersized
Undersized: Aria Operations limits undersized recommendations to 100% of the current allocation. For example, if a virtual machine
with 4 vCPUs consistently experiences high resource utilization, the recommendation is capped at adding 4 vCPUs instead of
suggesting 8.

[643]

Projection - Conservative Risk

In Aria Operations, capacity calculations can be adjusted based on the desired risk level, allowing you to customize the level of
conservatism in capacity planning. The figure shows the capacity calculations for a conservative risk level. The Conservative Risk Level
parameter influences the capacity engine's recommended resource size
recommended resource size, and we can modify it in the policy settings to match your
desired level of conservatism

Projection - Aggressive Risk
Aggressive Risk Level
Aggressive Risk Level
Capacity calculations at an aggressive risk level aim to maximize resource utilization and minimize overhead.
maximize resource utilization and minimize overhead. This setting assumes a
higher tolerance for risk and allows for more aggressive allocation of resources. It may result in higher consolidation ratios and tighter
resource utilization. The aggressive risk level settings can be adjusted in the Capacity policy settings, where you can specify the desired
level of aggressiveness.

Set Business Hours
Set Business Hours
Business Hours Schedule
Business Hours Schedule
Configure business hours for enhanced capacity analysis and projections in Aria Operations. Non-business hour activities on VMs, such
as OS upgrades or virus scans, can skew perceived idleness. By setting business hours, off-hour metrics can be effectively analyzed for
inventory, compliance, and troubleshooting. Analysis and recommendations for reclamation and rightsizing considers only these hours,
ignoring post-business hour spikes. Policies allow different objects to have varied business hours, which are reflected in the capacity
charts. After you specify business hours, the capacity forecast for the object will be based on the business hours and not 24 hours.

Business Hours.

click on the padlock.

Set All, this will apply the modified Business Hours to all days.

Capacity Thresholds and Metrics
Capacity Thresholds and Metrics
Capacity Thresholds and Metrics allows us to define when an alert should be triggered for a particular symptom based on the severity of
a condition. The default is 30 days for yellow (warning) and 10 days for red (critical). When it comes to Rightsizing, the Time Remaining
Time Remaining
Criticality
Criticality Threshold is used to control how much of the projected demand to consider when projecting the Recommended Size. The
Recommended Size
Recommended Size is determined by considering the peak projected demand between now and 30 days beyond the Time Remaining
Criticality Threshold. Since the default warning threshold is 30 days, that means Recommended Size also defaults to 60 days in the
future (30 days from the Time Remaining Criticality Threshold + 30 days).
The Recommended Size projection considers peak demand
Recommended Size projection considers peak demand within 30 days beyond the Criticality Threshold. We adjust appropriate
values as needed for our Rightsizing frequency, and set it to a value that gives us enough lead time to rightsize our VMs.

=== PAGE 590 ===
Module 12 - Enabling Chargeback for your Business (30 minutes) Advanced
Module 12 - Enabling Chargeback for your Business (30 minutes) Advanced
[663]
[663]
Introduction
Introduction
In VMware Aria Operations, financial management we are going to focus on the Chargeback mechanism. Key activities include setting
up Pricing Rate Cards and utilizing the associated dashboards.
There are two crucial processes in Aria Operations financial management: Showback and Chargeback.
• Showback
Showback gives an overview of the costs involved in operating IT infrastructure, such as the cost to run a VM. It's about the visibility
and accountability of the actual expenses incurred in providing these resources. It's the cost to the IT department to provide the
services.
• Chargeback
Chargeback, on the other hand, is about how much the IT department, as an internal service provider, charges back to the business
units (the consumers) for using those resources. It's essentially the "price tag" for the use of IT resources.
Simply put; A report showing the 'cost' can be seen as showback (e.g., the cost to run a VM on the infrastructure), while a report
showing the 'price' is chargeback (e.g., what you're charging the consumer for using a VM in your infrastructure).
Lab Captain(s):
Lab Captain(s):
•Bastian Beuggert, Lead Solution Architect, Switzerland

Rate Cards/Pricing
Rate Cards/Pricing

usage into actionable costs by assigning financial values to resources. The foundation of our cost data is the real-time metrics collected

Chargeback
Chargeback
Quantifying costs and implementing showback practices are vital in cloud-based IT environments. Let's have a look.
1. From the Cost page, under the Showback/Chargeback heading, Click Chargeback (vCenter).
hargeback (vCenter).
[689]
[689]
Price and Chargeback
Price and Chargeback
Accurately determining prices and implementing chargeback processes are crucial for billing customers based on their virtual machine
(VM) usage, including any additional fees or upcharges. This promotes transparency and enables customers to understand the costs
associated with their services.
Price
Price: Price represents the amount charged to customers for utilizing a VM. The price of a VM most often differ from its actual
operational costs, as additional charges or profit margins (upcharge) is included. Price considerations are specific to VMs, and
customers are typically billed on a monthly basis.
Chargeback
Chargeback: Involves generating a bill for customers based on the determined price for their VM usage. The bill should include a
breakdown of the charges associated with each VM, reflecting the price per month. Chargeback aims to provide customers with an
accurate representation of the costs incurred for their VMs and promotes transparency in billing practices.
HOL-2501-02-VCF-L_5.2: Getting More Out of It!
H A N D S - O N  L A B S  M A N U A L  |  6 1 1

=== PAGE 617 ===
[695]
[695]
Chargeback (VM Price) - Dashboard Overview
Chargeback (VM Price) - Dashboard Overview
The chargeback VM price dashboard lets you know how much we must spend to run a VM on behalf of your customer. In Aria
Operations, we configure the cost drivers and let the system automatically determine how much a VM costs based on your
infrastructure requirement. Cost Drivers
Cost Drivers cover server hardware, storage, licenses, application, maintenance, labor, network, facilities,
and additional costs configured within Aria Operations.
Price
Price is what you charge your customer for running their VM. The price of a VM can be based on the cost of the VM or based on a rate
card that you define. Prices can include up charges, service charges, and others.
HOL-2501-02-VCF-L_5.2: Getting More Out of It!

=== PAGE 618 ===
[696]
[696]
Month to date price
Month to date price
Price Summary of Selected Group shows the month to date price of the group. Month-to-date (MTD) Price calculates the price from the
time from the first day of the current month to the last completed business day before the current date not including the current date.

=== PAGE 621 ===
Based on a real life practical example
real life practical example, we've found that our third largest priced server is a Web server, representing 3.32% of the total
pricing structure. This serves as a starting point for further inquiries. We aim to determine why this specific server from our consumer
perspective has such a cost. Is the provider's charge excessive? Does the server genuinely consume 3.32% of resources, leading to the
elevated cost, which then impacts the price?".
Probing a server's price/cost is fundamental for delivering and receiving optimal value for both provider and consumer.
•Efficiency
Efficiency: Assuring provider costs match the value of the service.
•Resource Allocation
Resource Allocation: Optimizing what the provider offers, and the consumer receives.
•Transparency
Transparency: A clear billing strengthens the trust between provider and consumer.
•Insights
Insights: Highlighting areas for improvements.
•Strategy
Strategy: Inform about the future provider decisions.

Module 13 - Plan for your Future Capacity Needs (30 minutes) Intermediate
Module 13 - Plan for your Future Capacity Needs (30 minutes) Intermediate
[707]
[707]
Introduction
Introduction
Optimize Capacity with a What-If Scenario
Optimize Capacity with a What-If Scenario
A scenario is a situational model to determine a detailed estimation of the resources we must have available in the environment to
incorporate upcoming changes. We define scenarios that can potentially add resources to actual data centers. Aria Operations models
the scenario and calculates whether the desired workload can fit in the targeted data center. We can save multiple scenarios for
comparison or review.
Using the What-If
What-If method, we can plan for an increase or decrease in workload or capacity requirements in the virtual infrastructure. To
evaluate the demand and supply for capacity on the system objects, and to assess the potential risk to the current capacity, we can
create scenarios for adding and removing workloads. We can also determine how much capacity is required to make a migration work.
We can run one scenario or group scenarios and run them cumulatively.

Lab Captain(s):
•Bastian Beuggert, Lead Solution Architect, Switzerland

=== PAGE 633 ===
The Overview
Overview tab of the What-If
What-If analysis
analysis page has the following panes.
Each pane lets us run What-If
What-If scenarios
scenarios to optimize capacity based on workload, physical infrastructure, HCI nodes, or migration to the
cloud.
•Workload Planning: Traditional
Workload Planning: Traditional - Setup a scenario to 'deploy applications' by adding new workloads or 'deprecate
applications' by removing existing workloads. We can also evaluate the impact of 'reclaiming inefficient workloads' by using
the remove workload scenario.
•Infrastructure Planning: Traditional
Infrastructure Planning: Traditional - Determine the impact of adding or removing specific capacity in our environment. If
we're planning to upgrade the hosts in a cluster, setup a stacked scenario to 'Refresh hardware' with combination of
remove existing capacity from cluster and add new capacity to cluster.
•Migration Planning: VMware Cloud
Migration Planning: VMware Cloud - Evaluate the possibility of moving workloads and compare capacity and cost across
different VMware clouds. We can compare capacity and cost of workload across VMware Cloud for AWS(Amazon Web
Services), AVS(Azure VMware Solution), GCVE(Google Compute VMware Engine), VMware Cloud on Dell EMC, and OCVS
(Oracle Cloud VMware Solution)
•Datacenter Comparison: Private Cloud
Datacenter Comparison: Private Cloud - Evaluate the possibility of moving workloads across different datacenters and
clusters in the private cloud. We can compare cost of workloads across various datacenters so as to optimize and decide best
fitting datacenter for the workload under consideration.
•Workload Planning: Hyperconverged
Workload Planning: Hyperconverged - Plan for 'future workloads' to be deployed on vSAN environment with virtual machines
associated with specific storage policy related factors, such as Failures to Tolerate (FTT) and RAID level, to evaluate if the
usable capacity can cater to workloads to be deployed. We can also evaluate the impact of 'reclaiming inefficient workloads’
on vSAN cluster by using remove virtual machines scenario.
•Infrastructure Planning: Hyperconverged Infrastructure
Infrastructure Planning: Hyperconverged Infrastructure - Determine the impact of adding or removing specific
hyperconverged capacity to the vSAN environment. We can plan to add new vSAN ready nodes to the vSAN cluster to
evaluate the impact of increase in HCI capacity.
•Migration Planning: Public Cloud Migration
Migration Planning: Public Cloud Migration - Evaluate the possibility of moving workloads across different public clouds. We
can compare capacity and cost of workloads across vSphere, AWS, Azure, GCP, IBM Cloud by default or any cloud provider
of choice by uploading the rate card as prerequisite.

=== PAGE 663 ===
[743]
[743]
About Committed Scenarios
About Committed Scenarios
Before we move on, take a moment to read through this:
Committed Scenarios
Committed Scenarios:
•When we're sure that we need to reserve capacity, we can commit the scenario to have VMware Aria Operations set aside
resources for new, upcoming, or planned workloads.
•A committed scenario is a supposition about how the capacity and load change on our objects when we change the
conditions in our virtual infrastructure environment.
•We do not have to implement the changes that our committed scenario represents. By committing a scenario, we can
determine our capacity requirements before we implement the actual changes.
Why Create a Committed Scenario?
Why Create a Committed Scenario?
▪In organizations which have separate capacity management and operations teams, committing a scenario helps stakeholders
understand the current capacity and upcoming capacity requirements across the board.
▪With committed scenarios, capacity is reserved and this prevents the operations team from performing adhoc resource
increase on workloads, while the capacity manager is engaged in resource planning of new projects.
▪Committed Scenarios also helps the team responsible for infrastructure expansion, as it provides actionable insights into
future scenarios. In the event capacity becomes limited, it could be accounted for in the expansion
[744]
[744]
Committing our Scenario
Committing our Scenario
HOL-2501-02-VCF-L_5.2: Getting More Out of It!

=== PAGE 669 ===
Module 14 - Application Monitoring (45 minutes) Intermediate
Module 14 - Application Monitoring (45 minutes) Intermediate
[752]
[752]
Introduction
Introduction
Aria Operations
Aria Operations monitors the availability of your processes, services, and applications. We can leverage the Telegraf agent to monitor
the availability of multiple Linux processes and Windows services. Plus, VCF customers can use Aria Operations to monitor the
availability of their applications as VCF includes Aria Operations in the Enterprise Edition. Linux process monitoring
Linux process monitoring can be configured
to evaluate processes based on name, PID file, or via regular expressions for cases where processes may have a unique naming
scheme. Windows services
Windows services are configured via the service name.
Once Aria Operations has been configured to monitor these processes and services, it will begin collecting not only its status or running
state, but also basic utilization metrics such as CPU and Memory Usage. Aria Operations can also alert you when a service or process
goes down using predefined alerts.
Aria Operations supports native monitoring of applications using the Aria Operations Telegraf Agent. This works out of the box for most
applications, but do check out the documentation first as there may be additional requirements for some applications.
In this module, we will explore and compare Discover Services and Monitor Applications.
Discover Services:
Discover Services: Aria Operations can leverage the VMware Tools agent already installed and running on virtual machines to discover
and monitor processes and services, and to run basic OS commands and queries.
Monitor Applications:
Monitor Applications: Aria Operations can deploy the Telegraf agent to any managed virtual machines and can also collect metrics from
customer-deployed open-source Telegraf agents, including those running on bare metal servers.

[758]
Install Product-Managed Telegraf Agent
Install Product-Managed Telegraf Agent
[759]
[759]
About the Telegraf agent
About the Telegraf agent
The Telegraf agent is a versatile and efficient open source data collector widely used in monitoring systems, including VMware Aria
Operations.
It serves as a lightweight and flexible agent that collects and reports metrics from various sources. Its plugin-driven architecture,
lightweight nature, and flexibility in configuration make it a valuable component in monitoring and managing virtualized environments.
Since it is fairly efficient and customizable, it's also actively supported by a thriving community.
Open source Telegraf's source code is hosted on GitHub. InfluxData, the organization behind Telegraf, maintains an active community
where users can access forums, ask questions, and get support for Telegraf. Stack Overflow, the popular Q&A platform for developers,
has a dedicated Telegraf tag and Reddit's r/Telegraf subreddit hosts relevant sections and monitoring-focused forums.
We will take a look at how to install the Telegraf Agent easily using the Aria Operations User interface .
Please note that VMware Aria Operations offers two different flavor's of the Telegraf agent, the product-managed
product-managed agent and the open
open
source
source agent. For details refer to the official documentation What Are The Different Types of Telegraf Agents in VMware Aria
What Are The Different Types of Telegraf Agents in VMware Aria
Operations?
Operations?
Due to the dynamic nature of this lab environment and the host naming policy applied to deployed resources, the 6 numbers within the
name of a virtual machine may differ between the instructions provided, what is shown within the associated picture and the lab
environment.

=== PAGE 695 ===
[784]
[784]
Adding the Custom Script
Adding the Custom Script
Under the Manage Custom Scripts we are going to add a script located on the Linux server ubuntu-000308, that will return a value
back to VMware Aria Operations that returns the Number of SYSLOG errors in the last 7 days. In other environments you would
probably change that to 24 hours. See both scripts below
1. Make sure the Status is set to Activated
Activated.
2.Set Display name to syslog-error-count
3.Set the File Path to /opt/vmware/linux_err_count.sh
4.Set the Timeout to 50.
50.
5.Change the unit to Seconds.
Seconds.
6.Click SAVE
SAVE
Here is the Linux script counting SYSLOG errors for the last 24 hours
last 24 hours
Here we can see the Linux script counting SYSLOG errors for the last 7 days
last 7 days
#!/usr/bin/bash
# Number of SYSLOG errors in the last 24 hours
error_count=$(grep -i "error" /var/log/syslog | grep "$(date --date='24 hours ago' '+%b %e')" | wc 
echo $error_count

windows-000906

# Number of SYSLOG errors in the last 24 hours

=== PAGE 706 ===
[795]
[795]
Final Remarks
Final Remarks
By integrating the Telegraf agent with VMware Aria Operations and configuring Telegraf to output data in a format compatible with Aria
Operations, you can significantly enhance the platform's capabilities. This setup improves flexibility and scalability in environments with
diverse systems. Telegraf collects metrics from various sources and centralizes them in Aria Operations, providing a unified view of
operations across different platforms. This integration allows for effective event correlation and pattern identification, offering a broader
perspective rather than analyzing data in isolation.

Aria Operations:

Preferences menu.

Log Out.

=== PAGE 707 ===
[797]
[797]
Conclusion
Conclusion
In this module, we examined VMware Aria Operations ability to monitor processes
processes, services
services, and applications
applications, leveraging the Telegraf
Telegraf
agent for both Linux and Windows platforms. Aria Operations gathered crucial utilization metrics and initiated alerts for process or
service downtime. Native application monitoring was facilitated via the Aria Operations Telegraf Agent, with additional requirements for
some applications referenced in the documentation.
We explored the Discover Services
Discover Services and Monitor Applications
Monitor Applications functionalities. Discover Services employs the VMware Tools agent to
monitor processes and services, while Monitor Applications utilizes an open-source Telegraf agent for metric collection from managed
VMs. In summary, Discover Services offers more configuration information, whereas Monitor Applications provides a wider range of
performance metrics. The choice between these functionalities depends on the specific objectives of your operations.
[798]
[798]

=== PAGE 708 ===
Module 15 - Using VCF Diagnostics to Understand the Operational Health of your Private Cloud
Module 15 - Using VCF Diagnostics to Understand the Operational Health of your Private Cloud
(30 minutes) Intermediate
(30 minutes) Intermediate
[800]
[800]
Introduction
Introduction
Diagnostics for VMware Cloud Foundation is a centralized platform that monitors the overall operational status of the VMware Cloud
Foundation software stack.
It is a self-service platform that helps you analyze and troubleshoot the components ofVMware Cloud Foundation, including vCenter,
ESXi, vSAN, capabilities such as vSphere vMotion, snapshots, VM provisioning, and other issues including security advisories and
certificates. As an Infrastructure admin, you can monitor the operational state of your environment using diagnostics findings and
custom dashboards. The built-in dashboards are an extension to native VMware Aria Operations dashboards. Diagnostics validates if
your environment is up-to-date with the important VMware Security Advisories.
In this lab, we will review VCF Diagnostics in detail by walking though the various components (cards) and dashboards that work
together in Aria Operations to make this a valuable tool for any environment.
Lab Captain(s):
Lab Captain(s):
•Shannon Fitzpatrick - Staff Technical Adoption Manager, US
